--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion (-1.80) is a simple 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(yellow=4) with transitions: gray floor→stay(0.15), random→stay(0.22), black floor→stay(0.8)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(yellow=4) with transitions: gray floor→stay(0.95), random→stay(0.35), white floor→stay(0.7)

Wait - both states transition back to themselves (D=0 with 2 states means the only OTHER state). Let me re-read: with 2 states, leaving state 0, D=0 targets state 1. So:
- State 0 (GoToColor yellow): on gray→go to state 1, random→go to state 1, on black→go to state 1
- State 1 (GoAwayColor yellow): on gray→go to state 0, random→go to state 0, on white→go to state 0

So: GoToColor(yellow) until reaching black floor (source) or gray/random → switch to GoAwayColor(yellow), then GoAwayColor until gray/white/random → switch back.

This makes sense! GoToColor(yellow=4 which is the wall color?) actually... color 4 = yellow. But yellow isn't mentioned as a wall color. Hmm - maybe yellow LEDs on robots? The robots set their LEDs, so GoToColor might be tracking other robots' LED colors.

Actually `clr0 4` = yellow. With `cle1 0` (LED off), GoAwayColor(yellow) would repel from robots with yellow LEDs. State 0 has yellow LEDs and GoToColor(yellow) - robots attract to each other! State 1 has LEDs off and GoAwayColor(yellow) - avoids state-0 robots.

The logic: robots in state 0 cluster (GoToColor yellow = go toward yellow-LED robots), then when they hit black floor (source), they switch to GoAwayColor(yellow) = move away from the cluster = toward nest. On white (nest), they switch back.

**Strategy for Round 3:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: vary probabilities, try different LED colors, try GoToColor(other colors)
3. The 4-state machines ranked 2-4 also do well - exploit those
4. Try variations: what if state 1 uses GoToColor(red=1) to actively seek nest?

**Exploitation of champion:**
- Adjust transition probabilities (gray: 0.15→0.1/0.2, random: 0.22→0.15/0.3, black: 0.8→0.9/0.7)
- Try different colors for GoToColor in state 0 (robots could use cyan/magenta LEDs)
- Add a 3rd state for more explicit nest-seeking

**Exploration:**
- 2-state: GoToColor(cyan=6) + GoToColor(red=1) for nest
- 3-state explicit foraging: explore→go to source→return to nest
- Try with GoToColor targeting robot LED colors differently

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The champion (-1.81) is a 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(yellow=4), LED off, transitions: gray→s1(0.15), random→s1(0.22), black→s1(0.8), **white→s1(0.5)** (4th transition added!)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(yellow=4), LED off, transitions: gray→s0(0.95), random→s0(0.35), white→s0(0.7)

The crucial insight: **GoToColor(yellow=4) is not tracking other robots** (LEDs are off=0 in both states). Yellow (4) must be referring to something else in the environment - perhaps the YELLOW wall color! Wait, the walls behind the source are BLUE and GREEN. But GoToColor tracks colored objects visible to the camera...

Actually, with LEDs off, the robots aren't emitting yellow. But color 4=yellow might pick up arena features. Let me reconsider: perhaps yellow tracks some environmental feature, or the behavior degrades gracefully to exploration-like movement when no yellow is visible.

**What's working:**
- GoToColor(yellow=4) + GoAwayColor(yellow=4) with LED off
- Transition triggers: black floor (source found → switch to GoAway), white/gray floor (returning → switch to GoTo)
- The 4th white-floor transition in champion (p=0.5) slightly improved over the 3-transition version (-1.80)

**Observations on rankings:**
- Rank 6: state 1 = GoToColor(red=1) instead of GoAwayColor got -0.93 → still decent, red wall = nest!
- Rank 4: 3-state version with GoToColor(red) as state 2 got -1.23
- Rank 12: LED on (yellow) broke it → LED off is important
- Rank 13/14: exact champion topology without 4th transition scored 0.12 (different seed effects?) - actually these are the round 2 champion variants

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: try different probability combinations for the 4 transitions in state 0
3. Try adding a 3rd state (GoToColor red=1 for nest) to the champion structure
4. Try variations in state 1 transitions
5. Explore: what if we add a 4th transition to state 1 (black floor)?

Key parameter ranges that seem good:
- State 0 transitions: gray~0.15, random~0.22, black~0.8, white~0.4-0.6
- State 1 transitions: gray~0.95, random~0.3-0.4, white~0.65-0.75

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The champion (-2.05) is the 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(yellow=4), LED off, 4 transitions: black→s1(0.85), white→s1(0.7), random→s1(0.15), gray→s0(p=0.12) [D=0 with 3 states, leaving s0, targets state 1; D=1 targets state 2]

Wait, let me re-read: with 3 states, leaving state 0, D=0→state1, D=1→state2. So:
- n0x0: D=1→s1, c=0(black), p=0.85
- n0x1: D=1→s1, c=2(white), p=0.7  
- n0x2: D=1→s1, c=5(random), p=0.15
- n0x3: D=0→s0... wait D=0 from state 0 targets state 1 (the first other state)

Actually with 3 states (0,1,2), leaving state 0, others are [1,2]. D=0→state1, D=1→state2. So n0x3 D=0→state1, c=1(gray), p=0.12.

All state 0 transitions go to state 1! State 0 (GoToColor yellow) → State 1 (GoAwayColor yellow) when black/white/random/gray floor.

- State 1: GoAwayColor(yellow=4), transitions all go to s0: gray→s0(0.95), random→s0(0.35), white→s0(0.7)
- State 2: GoToColor(red=1), transitions: white→s1(0.8), gray→s0(0.5)

So state 2 navigates toward the red wall (nest!). When white floor (nest reached), go back to GoAway. When gray, go back to GoToColor(yellow).

**What the champion does:**
1. GoToColor(yellow) - moves toward yellow objects (perhaps arena features or source area)
2. When reaching source (black/white/gray/random), switch to GoAwayColor(yellow)
3. GoAwayColor moves away, eventually reaches state 2 (via white floor)
4. GoToColor(red=nest wall) navigates to nest
5. When white (nest) → back to GoAway; when gray → back to GoToColor(yellow)

**Key insight**: The champion differs from rank 2 (-1.71) mainly in: champion has `p0x3=0.12` (gray→s1 with low prob) vs rank2 has `p0x3=0.1`, and champion state 1 has white→s0 (not s2), while rank2 state 1 has white→s1 (self-loop via D=1 from s1 = state 2).

**Strategy for Round 6:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: vary p0x3 (0.08-0.15), vary state 1 and state 2 parameters
3. Try adding perceive-yellow transition to champion's state 1
4. Try adding 4th transition to state 2
5. Try different state 2 behaviors (GoAwayColor red, Exploration)

So the 4-state machine:
1. GoToColor(yellow) → when black/white/random/gray: switch to GoAwayColor(yellow)
2. GoAwayColor(yellow) → on gray/random: back to s0; on white: go to s3 GoAwayColor(red)

4. GoAwayColor(red=1) → on black: go to s2 GoToColor(red); on gray: back to s0

This is a more complex foraging cycle. The key insight: state 3 GoAwayColor(red) from nest area helps robots disperse back toward source.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 7:**

The champion (-2.53) and rank 2 (-2.51) are very similar 4-state machines. The main difference:
- Champion: p0x0=0.9 (black→s1), p0x1=0.65 (white→s1)
- Rank 2: p0x0=0.85 (black→s1), p0x1=0.7 (white→s1), but state 2 has p2x0=0.75, p2x1=0.55, p2x2=0.35

The structure that works well:
- S0: GoToColor(yellow=4): black→s1(0.9), white→s1(0.65), random→s1(0.15), gray→s1(0.12)
- S1: GoAwayColor(yellow=4): gray→s0(0.95), random→s0(0.35), white→s3(0.7)
- S2: GoToColor(red=1): white→s3(0.8), gray→s0(0.5), black→s1(0.3)
- S3: GoAwayColor(red=1): black→s2(0.7), gray→s0(0.5)

There's a big gap between ranks 1-6 (around -1.88 to -2.53) and ranks 7+ (below -1.56). The key parameters to exploit:
- p0x0: 0.85-0.95 (black→s1 from GoToColor)
- p0x1: 0.60-0.72 (white→s1)
- p1x2: 0.65-0.75 (white→s3 from GoAwayColor)
- p2x0: 0.75-0.85 (white→s3 from GoToColor red)
- p2x2: 0.25-0.35 (black→s1 from GoToColor red)

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit top performers with small parameter variations
3. Try combining champion's p0x0=0.9/p0x1=0.65 with rank2's state 2 parameters
4. Add 4th transition to state 0 (perceive yellow) or state 3
5. Try different white→s3 probability in state 1
6. Try slightly different random probability in state 1

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
